Cover and inside cover photo's by Mark Shearman

Front page: Amy Spencer is pictured competing in the Golden Jubilee Cup Final of 2002. What an outstanding talent! BBC Young Sports Personality of 2001; U20 AAA 200m Champion while only 16; silver medallist in the World Youth Games; senior women's indoor AAA champion over 200m; AAA U20 300m indoor Champion; Senior Inter-Counties winner over 200m; silver medallist in AAA U20 100m (behind our own Vernicha James) and winner at 200m; 7th in World U20 Champs. at 200m (won by Vernicha James) and bronze and silver medallist in both relays; British U20 Indoor 200m record holder; selected to represent Britain in the senior World Indoor Championships. All this and she is still only 17 years old. Not surprisingly she was awarded the Bill Merryman Trophy for the Young Belgravian of the Year in 2002.

Amy SpencerVernicha JamesMark Shearman

Photograph · page 2

Cover and inside cover photo's by Mark Shearman

Inside front cover: Top left: Emma Davies improved the club record for 800 metres to 2:03.93 when running to 6th in her semi-final at the Commonwealth Games in 2002. Here she is pictured taking maximum points over the same distance in the Jubilee Gold Cup Final.

Top right: Shelley Newman also improved her club record in 2002 - taking the discus mark out to 60.29m. She took the bronze medal in the Commonwealth Games in Manchester, was a finalist in the European Championships in Munich, and still found time to compete in Southern League and Cup Final teams.

Centre left: Goldie Sayes is pictured competing in the Commonwealth Games where she placed 6th. She set a new club mark of 58.20m for the javelin when winning the AAA U23 Championships and was another winner in the Cup Final.

Centre right: Yet another competitor in the Manchester Games, long jumper/sprinter/hurdler Sarah Claxton scored heavily for the club in Southern League competition. She has had a terrific indoor season, reducing the club record for 60m hurdles to 8.12 in winning the AAA Championship.

Bottom left: A huge putt of 15.57 won Eva Massey the Northern Irish shot championship but this winter she has launched the 4kg ball out to 16.32 so there could be much more in store this summer.

Bottom right: One of Belgrave's growing band of middle distance runners, Julie Mitchell twice broke the club record for 1500m (although that record is now owned by Catherine Berry). Julie placed 2nd in the Golden Jubilee Cup Final 1500m.

Editorial · page 3

The mammoth is still not extinct! Members who are as long in the teeth as the aforementioned beast may remember that these words were used by a former editor of the Belgravian after a similar lapse in our club magazine's appearance.

We can only apologise for the two year hiatus, especially because the lack of news has coincided with what has possibly been the most exciting and successful period in our club's history, a time when there has been more news than ever to share with our members. That very success is one of the reasons for the magazine's non-appearance. Our achievements as a club draw ever more heavily upon the time of our committee members and team managers - the same people who contribute towards this gazette. Some times, some things, have to give way.

But hopefully this situation should improve in the near future. Rex Bale (as usual a person already busy with other club work) has offered to assist and we hope to try to get back to bringing you news and results twice a year. Whether these will both be glossy colour editions we are not yet sure. Cost will obviously be a factor that we have to consider.

For this publication there was no way that we could bring you full reports on more than a few of the successes since the last issue. Just a small selection have been included but your Committee did feel that it was important that we recorded the bare results. So no blow by blow accounts, then, of our first ever AAA Half Marathon team win, our National and Southern six-stage relay victories, our record-breaking runs in the Southern 12-Stage, the tremendous surge in the success of our women's teams on road and track, the outstanding performances of individuals at World, European and Commonwealth Games - but the results are all here for posterity. Sadly, a gap of two years means that we also have far more than the normal number of obituaries to bring you. Not an ideal format for a magazine then, but at least we can present a clean start for the next publication.

For those who have access to the Internet, of course, there has been no lack of information, as our website - to be found at www.belgraveharriers.com - is updated with results, reports and statistics almost daily. We are now receiving around 100 visits per day on average - from points all over the globe. Dozens of one-time Belgravians from Australia, New Zealand and the USA have contacted us to let us know how pleased they are to be able to log on to see what the club is up to. Analysis of our visitors' locations shows that we have regular interest from practically every time-zone in each continent.

Rex Bale

Report · page 4

Hon. Secretary's Report to A.G.M. 2002

Alan Mead, Acting General Secretary

The last twelve months must have been the most successful in our 115 year history - but we have suffered a few setbacks and were shocked in July by the death of our General Secretary and Past President Bill Couzens. Ever present at our functions Bill will be sorely missed for a long time.

The tone for the next half decade was set in April when our Sponsorship & Development Committee negotiated a deal with adidas who, for the first time ever, committed themselves to a partnership with a club athletics team. We thank adidas and trust that we are providing the additional exposure they seek.

Two Belgravians were elected to Presidential office with athletic associations during the past year: Pam Davies was President of the Surrey County Athletic Association and Alan Black was President of the South of Thames Cross Country Association.

Recently we were pleased to present a South of England AA long service award to Johnny Morris who has officiated for us at race walking, road & cross country events and track & field competitions for more years than we care to recall.

And while thinking of some of those who have done so much work for us over the years, we should formally record our thanks to two people who have contributed greatly to the development of our club during the last twenty years. Life Member Sue Porter was our Secretary for some considerable time and Harry Porter, apart from being a Past President, has been an outstanding figure in the development of our women's section; both have also been very involved in our social activities; both have regularly and over a long period officiated for us at all types of events and in particular have been our starter and marksman at countless track and field meetings. The reason for recording these thanks now is that they are planning a move to France.

In track & field our most notable team success was the winning of the first ever combined men's and women's cup competition. So dominant were we in the final that we won the Golden Jubilee Cup by a huge margin and both our male and female athletes would have won the old single gender competitions outright. We can now point to our women's track and field team being the strongest in the country at one to score per event and would be justified in claiming the right for our women to join our men in representing the UK in the European Clubs' Cup in 2003.

And speaking of the European Cup for Champion Clubs, although our men's team was not at its very strongest in Lisbon, we nevertheless placed 6th in Group A, facing teams that were virtually national squads. This was the 7th time we have competed in Europe.

In the Women's Southern League our team once again earned promotion and next season will be competing in Division One where the opposition will be seriously stronger and we face the final barriers that stand between our women's team and the UK Women's Track & Field League.

As for the men, in spite of the draw on our resources by the Commonwealth Games and European Championships, we again proved ourselves to be the most powerful team by far in the British Athletics League, winning Division One for a sixth successive year and the eighth time all told. One more win and we will have held the title more times than any other club.

Here we should remind ourselves of the part played by Bill Laws in managing our British League Team. This year has seen his 20th season as BAL Team Manager - two decades that have given us unprecedented success and which have shown other sections of our club what can be done when one is committed to achieving goals. It is doubtful whether there is any other club in our sport that has a man with such a managerial record. In the annual club rankings, back at the beginning of his tenure, we were rated at 35th men's team; these lists have long been discontinued but there surely would be no argument that, thanks to Bill and the people who have worked with him, we are now number 1.

In the Men's Southern League we were unable to turn out teams to do justice to our depth and as a result have been relegated to Division Two.

As with the Men's Southern League, the younger male teams, too, found themselves stalling. After winning the National Junior League final two seasons ago and narrowly missing winning in 2001, this summer has found us at the bottom of the Southern Premier Division, suffering simply from a drop in the number of available athletes. The National Young Athletes team, promoted to the Southern Premier Division at the beginning of the season similarly found themselves short of competitors and have also ended the year in the relegation zone. It is interesting that it is in the younger age group of our successful Women's Southern League team that we face the most pressure and it is obvious that if we wish to maintain our intake of athletes of this age then we need, as a club, to put in some serious work.

Race Walking, we are told, is now in danger of being dropped from the Olympic Games. We cannot deny that names on the result sheets up and down the country bear a great similarity to those of twenty, thirty, even fifty years ago. Some young people are attracted into the sport but by and large they are not there by the time they reach their 'teens. Race Walking is highly regarded by other nations but in the UK, without a more alluring image it is difficult to see how new enthusiasts can be attracted. In the face of all of this our walkers continue to enjoy their sport - and pick up the occasional prizes as well.

In Road Running we have managed to emulate the success gained on the track and in Cross Country we are also making great strides. Soon after our last AGM we placed 3rd in the men's Southern 6-Stage Road Relay, medallists in this event for the first time, and a month later we pulled off a shock win in the AAA 6-Stage race at Sutton Coldfield; of all the road relays this is the hardest to win. It wasn't only our men who shocked as the women's 4-Stage team placed 7th in their first try at a AAA event. Unofficially, with the combined positions of our men's and women's teams, we were top road running club of the day.

Switching to the Country, the men regained the Surrey Cross Country League Division One title - with 40 different men turning out in the four matches - and the women won their series to earn promotion to Division One. At the National Cross Country Championships the men placed in the top ten for the third successive year and Allen Graffin won our first individual medal since 1948, placing second.

We have come out of a period which saw the demise of our cross country championships and have been able to inaugurate competitions for our men and women based on the six major cross country events and have incorporated our men's cross country championship into the SEAA's 9 mile race.

Back to the Road and in the Spring at Bath our senior women won national team medals - silver - in the AAA & UK Half Marathon Championship. In the same event the men became AAA & UK Champions, earning selection to represent the UK in the European Clubs Half Marathon Championship in Almada, Portugal. The Solent 5k was the next race to fall to Belgrave with a record low score and we won the Thames Valley Harriers Road Relay for the first time in 51 years, in record time. The Southern 12-stage was last won by us in 1991 at Wimbledon; now we won it again, at Milton Keynes, once more in a record time, and also provided the first B team. The women's 6-Stage team placed 4th from 35 clubs having been "in the medals" for half the race.

So, to the AAA "National" 12-Stage Road Relay, where a Belgrave onslaught saw us win by a record margin; we were just short of six and a half minutes ahead of the second team. We have had our hands on the National "London-Brighton" trophy before - in 1951 - when participation was on on a much more limited basis. Both long and short stage fastest times went to Belgrave athletes Allen and Andy Graffin. As is now often the case, the women's team were not far short of the men's performance, placing 5th and leading at the end of the first stage.

To finish with Road Running, we were second in the men's AAA 10 km Championship at Bradford.

Just as our team performances reached new heights, so too did our individual athletes.

A dozen medallists at the AAA Championships included two winners - Mick Jones in the hammer and Phillips Idowu in triple jump.

Vernicha James became our first global champion since John Regis when she won the World Junior 200 metre Championship in Jamaica. Incredibly, Amy Spencer, at 16 years of age, was also in the final of that race and both won silver and bronze medals in the GB relay squads.

Twenty seven events at the Commonwealth Games featured Belgrave athletes - 17 men of whom 10 reached the finals and 10 women including 7 finalists. Shelley Newman took bronze in the discus; bronze went to Vernicha James who ran in the sprint relay; high jumper Ben Challenger began to scale the heights again - and won bronze; Phillips Idowu produced a massive leap in the triple jump to lead the competition but ultimately take silver, Sean Baldock collected gold as part of the 4 x 400 metres squad; Mick Jones, one of the most popular of athletes was delighted - as were we - when he won the hammer throw gold.

Eight Belgravians represented the United Kingdom in Munich in the European Championships. Shelley Newman and Dwain Chambers made it to their finals - and Dwain won his first major games title with 9.96 in the 100 metres.

Six times Dwain Chambers came up against Maurice Greene, at that time the World Record holder, and five times the Belgrave man came out on top. Had Dwain not lost to the USA's Tim Montgomery in that World Record breaking race at the IAAF GP Final, then he would have been officially rated no. 1 in the world. As it is, he is clearly no. 2, having equalled the European and UK Records with 9.87.

Our senior men's club records are mature standards and difficult to improve but twice Chambers trimmed his own 100m best, twice Phillips Idowu did the same in the triple jump, taking it to 17.68m and the second best in the world for 2002, and Andy Graffin improved our indoor 1 mile time to 3:57.48.

Almost every one of our senior women's track & field indoor and outdoor records were broken in 2002. A total of 43 improvements

In 1993 at the AGM we challenged ourselves that around the turn of the millennium we would consistently turn out winning teams across the spectrum of athletics; that we would put in place an administration that could support these teams; that we would provide team managers who could take these teams to compete at the very highest levels, nationally and in Europe. We wanted future Belgravians 50 years hence to look back to this time with the same respect for the men and women who achieved these goals, as we have for those who took the club to national titles in the 1930s, 40s and early 50s.

Surely we have mostly achieved those aims and can now afford a moment to congratulate and thank one another.

In spite of this success there is still, now, much to do. We will always need more administrators, more coaches, more team managers, more officials, and perhaps most important of all a muster of young bloods prepared to take up the reins when their time comes. Oh! so slowly there are signs that at least some of these needs are being addressed.

Alan Mead, Acting General Secretary, 18 September 2002

Report · page 6

2003 - A critical year for the Belles

Bill Laws

News

Three years ago athletics' governing bodies decided to change the competition format for the Cup Competitions. Out were the separate men's and women's matches and in was a combined fixture. The inaugural competition took place in 2002 - the Queen's Jubilee Year - and not surprisingly was named the Golden Jubilee Cup.

On the face of it Belgrave's all conquering men's team was expected to be considerably weakened by what was considered to be a less competitive women's section. Faced with this challenge Belgrave's committee decided to strengthen the women's team and the flash of inspiration was to appoint Phil Green as overseas competitions manager charged with the task of getting our International standard women into overseas matches. Phil already had widespread contacts with most of the European meeting's organisers and Belgrave sent him to the International Fixtures Congresses in Moscow (2001) and Florence (2002). It was from these meetings that Phil negotiated invitations to over 50 overseas meets for Belgrave athletes seeking top level competition.

So as our senior and junior women ensured promotion in 2002 to the Division 1 of the Southern League many of our senior women competed in a number of European meetings. (A number of our men benefited in a similar way. The farthest travelled was Neil Speaight whom Phil took to the Boston indoor meet in February 2003).

So there we have it. An increasing number of International women members backing the Southern League promotion push while in parallel getting high quality races abroad - an opportunity seldom provided by our governing bodies.

And so in 2002, much to the surprise of the rule makers and our competitors, Belgrave's combined men's and women's team lifted the inaugural Golden Jubilee Cup by a huge margin. Even more importantly, separate scoring would also have given the men and the women outright victory under the old system. The plans had paid off!

But it is important that our women continue their progress into the British Women's League and so 2003 becomes critical for their aspirations. Promotion for the women this year has been set as the key priority for by the Belgrave Committee and increased support for the women will be provided as they press for another move up the competition ladder. Cynthia James, Bob Bridges, Jennifer Culley, Jacqui Smiter and Catherine Eastham are the management team and we wish them and their squad well in the season ahead.

Bill Laws

Phil GreenNeil SpeaightCynthia JamesBob BridgesJennifer CulleyJacqui SmiterCatherine EasthamBill Laws

Notice · page 6

Community Amateur Sports Club

Belgrave Harriers is now registered, with the Inland Revenue, as a Community Amateur Sports Club or CASC for short. This has come about as a result of the Budget in 2002 after much pressure from the sporting community for assistance to be given to registered clubs who may now be granted tax relief on donations received. Similar provisions already existed for such bodies as churches and approved charities but the latest initiative means that a CASC receives most of the advantages already available to those organizations without having to comply with the full requirements of the Charity Commissioners.

In effect if a person who pays income tax on income makes a donation to Belgrave, and signs a declaration to the effect that they are a taxable person, the Club will be able to claim a sum of 28p for every £1 received. Many members make donations during the year for which we are very grateful and we will be writing to you, in due course, seeking completion of a simple statement so that we can lodge a claim.

In addition to this relief any sum left to the Club, in a Will, is exempt from Inheritance Tax which has the effect of saving your estate 40% on such sums.

Notice · page 6

Belgrave launches new Promotions Company

Identifying future sources of income is one of the main topics under constant review by the Belgrave Committee. Our large number of competitive sections and increasing costs requires an annual funding budget of around £30,000.

A consequence of our new CASC status is that Belgrave has limited commercial freedom. The normally accepted solution - which Belgrave has adopted - is to set up a trading company with limited liabilities. Belgrave has taken steps which have resulted in the establishment of a wholly owned Limited Company.

The new company which is named Belgrave Sports Promotions Ltd. (BsportsP), is 100% owned by Belgrave Harriers. If for any reason the company ceases to trade this will have no effect on the ownership of Belgrave Hall or the finances of Belgrave Harriers. As the shares cannot be owned by the club as an entity, ownership is in the names of the club's four trustees - Bill Lucas DFC, Gordon Biscoe, Eric Hall and Carl Lawton. At a recent EGM unanimous approval was given to this arrangement whereby the trustees act on behalf of the club based on the decisions of the democratically elected Belgrave Committee.

Working capital is to be provided by a Belgrave member as an interest free loan. Executive directors will be appointed for a two year period. Each and every one of these directors will give of their time freely with the intention that the company will be profitable from year one.

A programme of projects and activities is being prepared. Any member wishing to get involved with this initiative and the future projects should make contact via Belgrave's Hon Sec - Roger Alsop.

Bill LucasGordon BiscoeEric HallCarl LawtonRoger Alsop

Notice · page 6

New Middle & Long Distance Track Series

A new middle and long distance track series is to be held this summer at Battersea Park on Wednesday evenings. Incorporated into meetings which will also feature some top class sprints and hurdles events, there will be five different races over the four evenings culminating in the SEAA 10,000m championships. The top prize is £75 to the runner who gains the most points according to the IAAF scoring tables from three of the four races. Due to the nature of the competition with it being based on times and not positions, it is expected to provide a good opportunity for runners to get some fast times in competitive races. Runners can enter individual races if they wish. A ladies series will also be held if there is sufficient interest, otherwise ladies can enter anyway and run in a mixed race.

Feature · page 7

H.T. Blackstaffe

Eric Hall

One of the results of having our own website is that it becomes a contact point for anyone seeking information on the Club and our members. Since the site was opened we have received a number of enquiries and with enhancements to the various search facilities these will, no doubt, increase.

Probably the most surprising enquiry to date is one which has had us reviewing the Club's history. It has always been accepted that the great long distance walker, Tommy Green, was our first, and to date, only, Olympic gold medallist when he triumphed in the 50 kilometre event back in 1932 in Los Angeles. While Tommy, without a shadow of a doubt, was the first athlete from the Club to win the highest award in our sport we had a man who won a gold medal back in 1908 and was our President from 1909 to 1912 - H.T. Blackstaffe.

News on the website relating to the cross country trophy donated by Mr Blackstaffe attracted the attention of someone researching the Blackstaffe family and so the story unfolded. The Official Report of the 1908 Games shows that H T was a member of Vesta Rowing Club, Putney, and weighed 11 stone 3 lbs when he competed in the Sculler's Race at Henley. The race reports make amusing reading as in his first heat Blackstaffe, wearing crimson and black, beat a Canadian "who ran into the piles twice, and upset on the second occasion. Blackstaffe waited for him the first time, and then sculled home alone." The next day, when a great race was expected, his German opponent had some trouble with his boat and failed to finish. So to the final on July 31st against home opposition in a very close race until the closing few yards when Blackstaffe went ahead in a "vigorous style." The report goes on to list the winner's achievements from 1897 and he was clearly, even at the age of 40, a remarkable sculler.

We have no idea when he joined Belgrave as the early minute books are missing but he was very involved in 1909 when he helped to fund the hire of a boxing instructor. He officiated in various capacities over the years until at least 1927. He had wide athletic interests as he was also, we believe, member of South London Harriers and, possibly, Blackheath which shows the close links between rowing and athletics in those early days.

Eric Hall

Photo from Vesta Rowing Club's collection

More on "H.T"

Harry Blackstaffe won over a hundred awards for rowing. He was a determined and persistent individual, which was evident in his attempt to win the Diamond Sculls at Henley (this event is a knockout individual contest, open to top international scullers from all over the world). Harry was a loser in the finals of the 1897, 1898, 1899 and 1905. He still persisted and in the following year 1906 he won the Diamond Sculls beating Capt. W.H. Darell, of the Household Brigade, in a time of 8 mins, 36 secs.

Harry went on to qualify to represent Great Britain in the Olympic games in 1908 as mentioned above where he won a gold medal in the Single Sculls in a time of 9:26.0. The British dominated the rowing events. Harry Blackstaffe overcame Alexander McCulloch only in the last 50 yards of the 1 1/2 mile race. The silver medal going to Alexander McCulloch, GBR and bronze to Bernhard von Gaza, GER.

Tommy GreenH.T. BlackstaffeHarry BlackstaffeW.H. DarellAlexander McCullochBernhard von GazaEric Hall

Column · page 7

From "The Belgravian"

75 years ago

One of the changes over the years has been that we no longer have 9 editions of the Belgravian as we had in 1928. Another difference is we no longer have Sunday strolls from Putney to Hampton Court - and back - plus the ever favoured all night stroll to Brighton. There were also concerts and whist drives with the Club Dinner in April at Clapham Junction.

On the sporting front we gained our first ever place, 3rd behind Reading and SLH, in the South of Thames at Redhill. We then went on to win the Mitcham to Brighton 10 stage relay by over 2 minutes but faired no better than 12th in the National London to Brighton a few weeks later.

Some things never change and it was announced that we had new Club vests and it is nice to read names which echo down the years - Tommy Green, Bill Webb, Ernie Duffett and Bert Footer, all still actively involved more than 40 years later. Another name to conjure with was Charlie Speechley but few who knew him would guess that he was showing his prowess, at that time, walking on the indoor circuit in the USA.

50 years ago

In 1953 the President was the same Ernie Duffett who was leading the runners home 25 years earlier.

Another 3rd in the South of Thames this time at Godalming and behind Bristol and HHH. This was followed by 3rd in the Southern and 6th in the National with the lead man on each occasion being Jack Brown with Charlie Walker, Len and Alan Bishop, along with Dennis Brickwood in the team.

Traditional features included the annual plea from the Treasurer for prompt payment of subs and a further appeal to support the Dinner. Road relay successes abounded and we were 4th in the "Brighton" while on the track one of the outstanding performers was Alan Sexton over the quarter mile. In a very classy meeting at Aylesbury we came out on top against Poly., TVH, Aylesford, and Achilles.

In the Poly Marathon there was "an amazing time" by Jim Peters and our team was led home by Geoff Pearson and Arthur Whitehead to finish 6th. Over a somewhat shorter distance Bob Taylor was 4th in the Southern AAA's 880 yards., Ted Gaworski was 2nd in the shot and 4th in discus. In the AAA's Jack Brown was 6th in the mile with the winner being Roger Bannister and at the same meeting Gordon Pirie set a new world six mile record of 28 mins 19.4 secs and went on to win the 3 miles the next day.

Our walkers were re-grouping at this time but, otherwise, the Club was having a very successful time in most events entered.

25 years ago

The fastest developing ladies walking section in the country and a 100 x 1 mile relay - these were a couple of the events of 1978. Ray Middleton, one of our greatest walkers was President, and a very active one, with the walkers having a very good year.

The Club auditor reported that we needed to cut costs or raise at least £1,000 per year to survive - what is that in today's money?

The Club ranking lists were headed by names such as Ernie Obeng, Snowy Brooks and John Bicourt. Ernie was in the process of taking sprinting in our club to an entirely new level, reducing the club record from 10.7 to 10.29 during the 1978 season.

Pleas were made for assistance in running the Youths and Boys sections and Leo Coy was having a hard time of it. Our man from adidas Mark Sinclair was then leading teams home to take the Boys Surrey Cross Country League with John Gladwin not too far behind.

The Seniors were not to be out done and they won their League also. In the National Cross Country John Bicourt "ran a blinder" to finish 17th at Leeds - which is to be the venue again in 2004.

Tommy GreenBill WebbErnie DuffettBert FooterCharlie SpeechleyJack BrownCharlie WalkerLenAlan BishopDennis BrickwoodAlan SextonJim PetersGeoff PearsonArthur WhiteheadBob TaylorTed GaworskiRoger BannisterGordon PirieRay MiddletonErnie ObengSnowy BrooksJohn BicourtLeo CoyMark SinclairJohn Gladwin

Feature · page 8

Cynthia James - Getting involved

Alan Mead

Cynthia James, President of Belgrave Harriers 2001-2002

Right: Cynthia doesn't always wear her Belgrave tracksuit!

It was so fitting that Cynthia's term as President of Belgrave Harriers turned out to be one of the most successful years in our club's 115 year history, for over and again in her life she has proved to herself that if one gets up and gets on with things, success is just around the corner. She also proves that in the parents of our young athletes there is a reservoir of talent, help, enthusiasm and drive which can be harnessed to continue to move our club forward - if we can only tap into it.

Athletics in England could not have been further from Cynthia's mind when she grew up in the West Indies in the 1950s. She was born in Port of Spain, Trinidad, in 1944, and was one of seven children born to Dolly and George. At Provident Girls High School she competed in the annual sports day events but her main sporting interest was netball at which she represented her school.

Like many a teenager she didn't know what she was going to do with herself when schooldays came to an end but eventually, when her mother said - "What about going to stay with your Auntie Monica in England for a while," Cynthia, who was up for anything, agreed. At seventeen years of age she packed her bags and swapped Port of Spain for London.

Living in Fulham and needing to earn a little pocket money, Cynthia took a job in a nearby factory where they were manufacturing fluorescent lights - and two years later she was still there, until eventually she said to herself, "if my mum could see me now, working in this place and wasting all my schooling!" So it was goodbye to the factory and "hello" to a career with British Telecom, where she began as a telephone operator on directory enquiries at the Chelsea exchange. This was much better. Within a year she became a senior operator, then moved on to become a supervisor at the Victoria Exchange and eventually a Unit Manager. By the end of her career Cynthia had become known as the "hatchet woman" as she travelled from exchange to exchange as she prepared them for closing down and replacement with new technology. Eventually the curtain fell on Cynthia's own career at BT as she too, after 26 years, was made redundant. Life was now very busy - not made less so as she had become somewhat involved with a local athletic club! But after a few months at home Cynthia got up to start on something new, although a little less pressured, and became a receptionist at the Royal Brompton Hospital in Sydney Street, Chelsea.

Back in her early days in Trinidad a young lad living at the end of her road had caught her eye. He played in a steel band. Years later she was invited to a party in Fulham and recognised one of the other guests as that same boy from Port of Spain. It was Ray James. They've now enjoyed 35 years of marriage together, have children Cindy and Richard and two grandchildren as well. Not only that, Ray's sister married one of Cynthia's brothers!

Cynthia's relations are now scattered across the world - Canada, the USA; her mother celebrated her 80th birthday last year back in Trinidad and Cynthia was pleased to be able to get back to join her; Dad is now charges-de-affairs for Jamaica in the city of Panama.

When 12 year-old Cindy showed early aptitude for athletics Cynthia began to look around for a group for her to join. Someone suggested that they went down to Battersea Park. In the depths of winter, Cynthia found herself sitting on a bench next to the track watching the girls being put through their paces by Harry Porter and Frank Simmons. It was obvious that these two guys had their hands full and in a very short time Cynthia was offering to help. Her attitude of getting involved soon led to a suggestion from Harry that maybe she'd like to 'phone up some of the younger girls before a match - as it sounded better coming from a woman. The first steps of team managership had been taken! And even better, the James gang came as a package, so Ray ended up officiating on the field, Cynthia running around with the numbers, Cindy was out setting records on track and field (some of them still stand today) and before long even young Richard found himself racing in Belgrave colours.

Now, twentyone years later, we can look back on the very valued help and friendship that this family has brought to our club. Cynthia took on ever more of the pre-match 'phone calls and hassles; ensuring that the girls got to their starting marks in time for their events; stepping in herself to heave a shot, discus or hammer should the need arise. She first became Joint Team Manager with Harry but now is in charge of one of the most exciting teams in athletics. She knew nothing of rules and declaration sheets when she started and readily acknowledges the help she has received from various people, especially Bob Bridges.

Cynthia hasn't confined herself to looking after her athletes and chasing about with her team sheet though. Countless times a thirsty track or field judge has had cause to thank her as she has provided seemingly unlimited supplies of tea, coffee and a veritable haystack of fruit cake and biscuits. It would be true to say that, thanks to Cynthia, we are now regarded as a club that always "look after their officials". It was not always so.

Remember that all this activity is not reserved for our women's team only (although originally she believed Belgrave to be a girls' club!) Our men's matches always run better for Cynthia's attendance and she soon has the lads carrying refreshments around - as meek as any lambs.

It is difficult to think of any club social event that has not been graced by Cynthia and Ray's presence in the last two decades. Cynthia's name was added to our Life Member's list in 1999 and for the period covering 2001 to 2002 we were delighted that she agreed to become our President.

Alan Mead

A three month old Cynthia, sits on Auntie Veda's knee

Cynthia JamesCynthiaDollyGeorgeAuntie MonicaAuntie VedaRay JamesCindyRichardHarry PorterFrank SimmonsBob BridgesAlan Mead

Feature · page 9

Tony Fairclough - an "A" team man

Gordon Biscoe and Alan Mead

Tony Fairclough, President of Belgrave Harriers 2002-2003

Track judging at Battersea, 2003

Back in the years of 1959 and 1960, Tom Carter, with the aid of Les Stokell, was gathering a group of young athletes together. They aimed, as The Belgravian of those days tells us, to find youngsters who "could be depended upon to train enthusiastically, to turn out for the club when required and in whom could be inculcated some of the traditions and 'esprit de corps' which made Belgrave such a power in the athletics world until a few years ago". The Belgrave net spread across South London and before long, with Bob Taylor helping on the coaching side and Jack Hampshire Snr. carrying out the admin there were the first stirrings of success.

A huge event that we promoted in those days was the Belgrave Open Youths Road Relay. Fifty or sixty teams of 15-16 year old boys from all over the country, including squads from the top teams of the day - Coventry Godiva, Sheffield United, Hallamshire Harriers - would gather under the starting banner in The Causeway for four stages of two and a half miles. In 1960 Belgrave had a good team and were hoping for a forward placing. Arthur Whitehead was race organiser and he had managed to get the legendary South African distance runner Jackie Mekler to start the event.

A terrific battle ensued on stage one with future international Alan Rushmer (then Oldbury A & CC but later Tipton H) getting beaten by Hallamshire's Evans who was close to the course record with 11 mins. 11 secs. But not so far behind was an unknown Belgrave Harrier who had been placed in the B team as his ability before the race had not been measured. Tony Fairclough, a young lad from Dulwich, ran 11:44 to be our second fastest of the day - and had he run in the A team we would most probably have won bronze medals.

Tony's first choice of sport was football and he played for his school, William Penn, on the wing. Games afternoon and an energetic kick-about was always eagerly looked forward to but on one occasion the boys were told, "Sorry, no football today. The pitch is too wet. You can all run round the fields instead." There was much mumbling about this particular activity being a waste of time - but it was to set Tony off in a new direction. Without too much distress, when the football pitches had been circled, he placed 2nd to the South London Schools Cross Country Champion and was immediately approached by the Games Master. "Ok Fairclough, you're in the school's cross-country team for the district championships. Tony didn't fancy it much - but in those days one did as one was told. He ran - and placed 3rd.

Young Fairclough's Mum was proud of her son and as she told her colleagues in the office of his prowess as a runner and that maybe he should join a running club, a chap there immediately took notice and said, "He should come and run at my club. Get him to go up to Belgrave Hall in Wimbledon and ask for Tom Carter. It was Terry, the shorter distance half of the racing Newell brothers. Both Pat and Terry Newell are still with us to this day.

For every race after that initial road relay, Tony was an A team man. Along with half a dozen or more lads he was picked up by Tom Carter on Saturday afternoons and they all piled into Tom's Bedford Dormobile builder's van, sitting on loose benches in the back as they were driven to county and area cross country championships and road relays.

The rest, as they say, is history. Tony's cross country achievements ranged from an early 14th in his first Surrey Youth's CC Race in 1961 to a best position of 36th in the senior National CC Champs of 1969 at Parliament Hill. As a junior he was 4th in the Surrey, 3rd in the Southern, 5th in the National and 19th in the Inter-Counties - all in 1964 - and that year he was awarded a senior ECCU vest. On the road he was a regular in our road relay teams, setting a record for stage 6 in the London to Brighton in 1963 - and as the race was taken off the Brighton Road shortly afterwards we presume this mark still stands today! He was club champion in our junior 5 miles and senior 10 miles cross country races and at 6 miles on the track and our "Five and three quarter" road race.

For as long as we have known Tony, we have also known Gill. They married in 1965 and a year later moved to Gillingham where Tony ran second-claim for Medway AC. They have three daughters - Karen, Joanne and Stella. Tony worked for BP at the refinery on the Isle of Grain, until it closed in 1984, and later went back to work at the container port from 1989 until very recently. He has now retired.

The Fairclough talent for running blossomed again in Joanne who soon joined in at the local athletic club and did so well that she won an athletic scholarship to Iowa State University in the USA. Having a daughter involved in their favourite sport of course resulted in Mum and Dad going along and before long they found themselves acting as officials. Tony became a qualified track judge in 1990 while Gillian did likewise as a field judge. Both have "risen through the ranks" to become respected Grade II officials working at track and field meetings for Kent County and Belgrave in the summer and for the county and the South of England AA in the winter at cross country and road events.

As a runner Tony was a team managers delight - uncomplicated, reliable and always giving 100% effort. His quiet and unassuming disposition belied the tenacity he applied to his running. It's over 40 years since that unknown lad raised a few eyebrows at our Youths Road Relay. Although he has lived in Kent for most of that time he has always maintained his link with Belgrave and continues to be a loyal Club member and a great ambassador, not only for Belgrave, but for our sport in general.

Gordon Biscoe and Alan Mead

Right: Tony runs to 18th place in the Southern Junior CC Champs of 1963.

Report · page 10

What a thriller! Bels. miss National gold medals by six seconds

A Tale of Two Relays

AAA "National" 12-Stage Road Relay, Sutton Coldfield, 28 April 2001

Well, we knew it was going to be exciting but who could have foreseen such a story unfolding. Team and spectators alike experienced every emotion throughout the 4 hour saga and then came that final devastating, gut-wrenching twist. As reserve Will Cockerell said afterwards, "It was almost unbelievable: but of course it wasn't unbelievable at all - just sport at its cruellest".

After an appalling start, a nigh-on miraculous fight-back saw us head the field for five stages - more than any other club on the day - only for the closest ever finish at Sutton Park to go against us. A blinding run from the last Tipton runner pushed the Bels. back to silver by just six seconds. It was very quiet inside the Belgrave tent for a while. The shock of having the prize snatched away just an instant after we believed for the first time that it was to be ours was stunning. This had been the target since the beginning of the season and we had come so close. But this group of runners is much more than a team. There is a feeling for each other that rival clubs do not see when they look at Belgrave from the outside. There is no blame, anyone can have a bad run just as they can have a good one - and within 30 minutes there were already the signs of looking forward. Spencer Barden was soon upbeat: "Don't worry. Next year we'll do it".

Times were affected by an icy wind which became stronger throughout the afternoon, bringing with it pulses of rain and sleet. Short stage times were down by about 10 seconds - long stages perhaps half as much again. There were 69 teams in the initial charge down the hill; 64 finished.

Stage 1. Kassa Tadesse was back as a full-time athlete having put his restaurant owning aspirations on hold for a few years, his target (later achieved) a British passport and a place in the GB half-marathon team. Six weeks of training in Kenya with John Mutai's coach had seen him shed pounds and he looked the gaunt Kassa of 1994 where the first stage on this course had seen him come home in 1st place. Could he do it again? He hadn't raced for around two years and had arrived home from Nairobi only the day before.

Early reports over the tannoy told us that Kassa was lying in third place ... was in the leading bunch ... but he wasn't in sight when at the end of the stage the Swansea man led the field through the takeover zone ... or one minute later when the first dozen teams were through ... or after a further minute when we were into the thirties. It was "head in hands" time for the team-manager. Was Kassa injured? Perhaps he wasn't going to finish at all and eleven Belgrave runners were going to be left stranded, their day finished before it had begun.

Eventually, looking awful, Kassa came through in 35th. It transpired that he had stopped three times to vomit on the second half of the circuit and, as he barely kept moving over the last kilometre, runners had streamed past him. He was some two minutes slower than anticipated - not the sort of start we were looking for, two and a quarter minutes off the lead.

1 Swansea 25:50; 2 Trafford 25:52; 3 Mansfield 26:12; 35 Belgrave 28:06. Fastest: J.Lewis (Swansea) 25:50; R.Gould (Trafford) 25:52; B.Farquarson (Mansfield) 26:12; K.Tadesse 28:06 (35th).

Stage 2. Well at least it was all set up nicely for Richard Ward, youngest man in the team. He'd been warned of the possibility of receiving a forward placing and being in the firing line but here was a different scene and he adapted to it well. It was like some spectacular "shoot 'em up" video game. In the dog-fight of this first short stage he picked off man after man whilst being subjected to the blast of runners like Telford AC's Chris Davies who came through from 41st to 14th with the day's fastest of 14:03. Richard thrived in all this action, taking the Bels up 15 places to clock our fastest of the day - the stage's =6th best and =9th of the day. He enjoyed himself so much that he tells us that he has every intention of making this race a priority for the next 20 years!

Up front the race favourites Salford and Tipton were already well established in 3rd and 4th places and, in spite of Richard's efforts, we were still 2:09 away from the leaders Trafford.

1 Trafford 40:37; 2 Bromsgrove & Redditch 41:09; 3 Salford 41:18; 20 Belgrave 42:46. Fastest: C.Davies (Telford) 14:03; J.Moorehouse (Birchfield) 14:15; J.Bowler (Bromsgrove & Redditch) 14:17; R.Ward 14:40 (=6th).

Stage 3. Tipton, Salford and Birchfield moved into the first three places on this second long stage and even seasoned onlookers could have been forgiven for assuming that the medallists had already sorted themselves out. Paul Freary thought otherwise: "Ok. So it was a bad start but we've got another 10 stages to go yet. We can get up there." As good as his word, Paul smoothly weaved his way through the field and in a time just 1 second faster than his run of the previous year claimed 10 scalps and we went into the top ten, 1:14 down on the men from the Black Country. This was to have been Paul Evans' stage but our veteran marathoner had had to pull out of the line-up with two days to go, due to post-London calf muscle problems.

1 Tipton 1:07:43; 2 Salford 1:07:50; 3 Birchfield 1:08:11; 10 Belgrave 1:09:24. Fastest: G.Tromans (Coventry) 26:01; B.Whitby (Windsor SE&H) 26:04; D.Taylor (Blackheath) 26:14; P.Freary 26:38 (10th).

Stage 4. Twelve months earlier 21 year-old Rick Hayman had barely started running and now, as reserve, he grabbed his chance to go into the team. An 8:47 track 3,000 metres during the week had given him confidence and he kept the forward momentum going by overtaking the blue and white vested Bedford man to move us into 9th. Announcer Bud Baldaro reminded the crowd that Belgrave had some strong long stage runners to come and knowing that some of those ahead would run out of steam soon, we began to dream again of medals. Of our Southern rivals, Windsor were now in 4th, Aldershot 6th, Bedford 12th and Shaftesbury 15th.

1 Tipton 1:22:46; 2 Salford 1:22:51; 3 Birchfield 1:23:22; 9 Belgrave 1:24:41. Fastest: G.Cuddy (Sale) 14:30; M.Dalkins (Stoke) 14:48; R.Scanlon (Coventry) 14:49; R.Hayman 15:17 (19th).

Stage 5. There were some mean men on stage 5. Morpeth had been languishing down the field but now turned out Ian Hudspith. Birchfield's Rob Birchall was entering the fray as was Blackheath's first Brit home in the London Marathon, Mark Steinle. What impression could our Olympic steeplechaser Justin Chaston make in this company, with 5 hours of jet-lag in his system? The answer: a good one. Twice a stitch caused him to ease right off but he became our fastest long stage man so far and was third best on the stage while overtaking Aldershot and Sale. Birchfield had grabbed a 36 second lead and we were now 2nd Southern club behind Windsor SE&H, although still an agonising 1:09 off the lead.

1 Birchfield 1:49:18; 2,Tipton 1:49:54; 3 Salford 1:50:13; 7 Belgrave 1:51:03. Fastest: I.Hudspith (Morpeth) 25:55; R.Birchall (Birchfield) 25:56; J.Chaston 26:22.

Stage 6. Taking us to the half-way point was "Gnasher". Kevin Nash's coach Richard Partridge has had some glory days in Sutton Park himself and was on hand to see his protégé have a cracking run - fastest of the stage - to dispose of Windsor SE&H, Swansea and Trafford. An absolutely amazing recovery! Fourth place was now ours and the medal positions were lined up a mere 16, 22 and 49 seconds ahead with Birchfield rumoured to be running out of top men and Tipton moving back into the lead. What a race!

1 Tipton 2:04:56; 2 Salford 2:05:23; 3 Birchfield 2:05:29; 4 Belgrave 2:05:45. Fastest: K.Nash (Belgrave) 14:42; C.Low (Shaftesbury) 14:51; N.Caddy (Tipton) 15:02.

Stage 7. Mark Miles is an awesome runner. Two years previously, at the SEAA 12-Stage, officials suggested that perhaps Mark had cut the course, so fast was his time. Co-holder of our fastest short stage time around Sutton Park (13:49) he had agreed to run a long stage if we thought it would make the difference between winning or not. We did think so - and "Milesy" went long.

Seemingly flying with feet barely touching the ground, Mark ate up Birchfield and Salford and then set about Tipton's GB half marathon international Nick Jones. Lead motor bike, Jones and then Mark disappeared from the view, heading toward the furthest reach of the long stage. All eyes peered into the distance for their return. Yes. There was the headlight of the bike and the runner behind was ... wearing a claret vest with gold hoop. We were in the lead! Ten seconds clear at the take-over, Mark was fastest long stage man of the day and second on our own all-time list only to Paul Evans.

1 Belgrave 2:31:30; 2 Tipton 2:31:40; 3 Birchfield 2:32:21. Fastest: M.Miles (Belgrave) 25:45; J.Wild (Sale) 25:58; E.DeJonge (Shaftesbury) 26:09.

Stage 8. Talk about pressure! A pale looking Roger Alsop set off down the slope plumb in the sights of Tipton's Seb Shepley. "I'm not worried if he comes back in second," said next man Spencer Barden, "it'll give me someone to chase." Someone to chase? Sorry Spen. but no such luxury as Roger proceeded to put another 11 seconds between the Bels and their green and white vested rivals. A medal of some sort now seemed a certainty with fourth place a full 1 minute and 19 seconds behind us.

1 Belgrave 2:46:36; 2 Tipton 2:46:57; 3 Birchfield 2:47:16. Fastest: I.Gillespie (Birchfield) 14:55; R.Alsop (Belgrave) 15:06; S.Shepley (Tipton) 15:17.

Stage 9. A confident Spencer Barden with a successful winter behind him had returned from warm weather training in Portugal itching to get into this race. We needed a reasonable buffer going into the last quarter of the race - something close to a minute would be nice - and surely Spen was the man to give it to us. Working hard and setting the fastest time for the lap he levered that gap from 21 seconds up to 49. That could be just enough but my goodness this was going to be close.

1 Belgrave 3:12:56; 2 Tipton 3:13:45; 3 Birchfield 3:14:06. Fastest: S.Barden 26:20; W.Burns (Salford) 26:46; R.Mayock (Sale) 26:48.

Stage 10. Another short stage man in the firing line: Rob McHarg had started the week looking forward to being reserve and now found himself on the tarmac, in the team, in the lead. Every second he could hold on to was crucial and he did a fine job. Tipton came up to within 15 seconds as their runner clocked a time that put him in the day's top ten. A minute further back the battle for the bronze medal was looking ferocious as a mere 6 seconds now covered Birchfield and Salford.

1 Belgrave 3:28:10; 2 Tipton 3:28:25; 3 Birchfield 3:29:36. Fastest: M.Hill (Tipton) 14:40; M.Moorhouse (Salford) 15:01; R.McHarg (Belgrave) 15:14.

Stage 11. The penultimate stage surely had to be the key. If Alaster Stewart could only stop Tipton's Nigel Stirk from getting past then man for man we believed that our own Lee Hurst would be able to look after the last leg. Stirk's pbs were somewhat faster than Al's but this was our man's 18th time of racing on the Sutton Park roadway. He knows the course like the back of his hand and it was like a dream coming true - in the lead and racing against Tipton Harriers for the most coveted trophy in Team Road Running.

The tension was really going up now. A 'phone check with supporters out on the course revealed that after a couple of kilometres the gap had gone up. Belgrave were now 20 seconds clear and if there was one thing we were sure of, even when well psyched up, metronome Al would never go off too fast.

Incredible. Far from stopping Stirk from getting by, at the end of the last long stage of the day Al had increased the lead by a further 20 seconds.

1 Belgrave 3:55:21; 2 Tipton 3:55:56; 3 Birchfield 3:57:25. Fastest: A.Stewart (Belgrave) 27:11; N.Stirk (Tipton) 27:31; C.Cariss (Bingley) 27:33.

Stage 12. The margin was 35 seconds. Tipton's Danny Gibbons was thought to be about on a par with our own Lee Hurst. For the first time, after almost four hours of racing, we felt fully confident that the race was ours. Photographer Mark Shearman said that he would be wanting to get the team together for a photograph and would our man be kind enough to celebrate as he went across the line. Our supporters began to come in from the course to witness the first ever Belgrave victory at Sutton Park. There was a little flutter when the announcer relayed the news that Gibbons had pulled back 5 seconds; but of course he had - he had nothing to lose - he had to go all out. Lee would be fine. What we didn't know was that Lee's heart had already been pumping in overdrive before he set out. It had been a long day of incredible tension.

Bud Baldaro's voice sounded alarm bells. "They are at Keeper's Pool and the Tipton man is just three seconds behind." Tipton supporters still spread around the course had been screaming themselves hoarse and their man was flying. The Belgrave team sprinted off down the finishing straight to give their support. Approaching the final turn Gibbons took the lead. As they turned into the straight Hurst went ahead again. Now the headlight of the lead bike could be seen by those at the finish - and the team immediately behind was - Tipton. Gibbons' arm went up in a victory salute. He was 5th fastest of the day. They'd got it. It was the closest finish in the history of the race.

We can't do better than return to the words of Will Cockerell (12th man at Milton Keynes) who knows what a last leg run is like.

"We all know, Lee could go back to Sutton Park tomorrow and run 15 to 20 seconds faster than he did today without breaking a sweat. But the pressure he was under - well, only he could know, I would have said it was four or five times the pressure that I had at Milton Keynes the other day. Half a mile into my stage that day, my legs had turned to jelly and that horrid feeling of nausea was overwhelming as I said to myself that if the Bedford man catches me, I'll have to let him go - I have no fight left. I shared these sentiments with Lee after his run and he said he had experienced every emotion that I was speaking of; and then some. He was running with the proverbial fridge on his back."

1 Tipton 4:10:27; 2 Belgrave 4:10:33; 3 Salford 4:12:34; 4 Birchfield 4:12:48; 5 Sale 4:14:53; 6 Shaftesbury 4:15:38. Fastest: D.Gibbons (Tipton) 14:31; K.Kyereme (Shaftesbury) 14:34; M.Farah (Windsor SE&H) 14:44; L.Hurst 15:12 (6th)

Fastest of the day. Long stages: 1 M.Miles (Belgrave) 25:45; 2 J.Lewis (Swansea) 25:50; 3 R.Gould (Trafford) 25:52; 4 I.Hudspith 25:55; 5 R.Birchall (Birchfield) 25:56; 6 J.Wild (Sale) 25:58. Short stages: 1 C.Davies (Telford) 14:03; 2 J.Moorhouse (Birchfield) 14:15; 3 J.Bowler (Bromsgrove & Redditch) 14:17; 4 G.Cuddy (Sale) 14:30; 5 D.Gibbons (Tipton) 14:31; 6 K.Kyereme (Shaftesbury) 14:34.

Report · page 12

The National 12 Stage title returns to Belgrave after 51 years

A Tale of Two Relays

AAA "National" 12-Stage Road Relay, Sutton Coldfield, 27 April 2002.

It was an historic and enjoyable day for Belgrave Harriers - but an exciting race it was not! The Bels. predictably took the lead with three laps gone and then spent the next three hours turning the screws to open up the greatest winning margin in the history of the race (6 minutes 26 seconds); but as the team manager told AW reporter Jason Henderson after the race, "We had enough excitement last year to last a lifetime!"

It was Belgrave's first win in the National 12 Stage since 1951 and apart from the pleasure in taking the magnificent "News of the World" trophy and twelve gold medals, Andy and Allen Graffin clocked the day's fastest short and long stages, and David Anderson and Steve Sharp backed up with second and third best short times while Mark Miles and Spencer Barden placed in the top six over the long lap.

It was a day to remember for a long, long time. Everything went to plan - even down to the provision of champagne for the post race celebrations, courtesy of Bill Laws and Gordon Biscoe. It has taken seven years of hard work to achieve our goal - but maybe that was the easy bit - staying at the top could be a lot tougher.

Conditions were very breezy with showers, becoming drier but windier as the race progressed. Short stages: 3 miles 8 yards; long stages 5 miles 706 yards.

Stage 1. Enjoying the chance to steer as clear of stage 12 as possible, Lee Hurst was given the target of a place in the teens and although he was a little disappointed with his time, his 17th spot in 27:13 - place and time - was exactly what had been predicted by Vice-Captain Al Stewart. Tipton had made a strong start to place 5th while Morpeth were just ahead of us in 15th.

Fastest: 1 A.O'Connor (Liverpool) 26:07; 2 C.Smith (London Irish) 26:12; 3 S.Bailey (Sale H Manchester) 26:18; 17 L.Hurst 27:13.

Stage 2. Richard Ward came into the team on Friday morning with the withdrawal of Kevin Nash due to a knee injury. Eager to prove that he should have been in the line-up from the outset, Richard grasped his chance and churned out a 14:43 to pick off eight of the opposition. What a substitute! Tipton had moved up to third but we were only 20 seconds behind.

1 Wells City H 41:06; 2 Sale H Manchester 41:26; 3 Tipton H 41:36; 9 Belgrave H 41:56. Fastest: 1 M.Openshaw (Birchfield) 14:12; =2 C.Livesey (Preston) and B.Whitby (Windsor SE&H) 14:16; 8 R.Ward 14:42.

Stage 3. Most Belgrave supporters predicted that we'd go ahead on stage four but Spencer Barden took full advantage of an earlier than normal leg to chase down the men up front. Ian Grimes of Newham & Essex Beagles was also having a good run but Spen was the man in charge and as he stormed the final rise even hardened campaigners went misty-eyed at the sight of a claret and gold vest heading the field right on cue. Nearly half a minute came off Spencer's best time for the course and the lead was 6 seconds. Our starters had done us proud - now what could the front runners do? Could they open the gap we wanted?

1 Belgrave H 1:07:48; 2 Newham & Essex Beagles 1:07:54; 3 Tipton H 1:08:45. Fastest: 1 S.Barden (Belgrave H) 25:52; 2 I.Grime (Newham & Essex B) 26:05; 3 M.Vaux Harvey (Kidderminster) 26:23.

Stage 4. As the wind rose and another squall swept the start area Andrew Graffin set out on his mission to break away from the field. With a string of indoor sub-4 minute miles under his belt this winter, no doubt he'd have like to have cracked 14 minutes. Sadly, in spite of the urging from announcer Bud Baldaro as our man approached the line, the digital clock flipped over to the "fourteens" before Mark Miles could be sent on his way. But the damage had been done and the lead was now 1 minute 44 seconds. For the first time our colours were out of sight of the chasing field and Andy's 14:03 was the target at which short-stage hopefuls would take aim.

1 Belgrave H 1:21:51; 2 Newham & Essex B 1:23:35; 3 Sale H Manchester 1:23:54. Fastest: 1 Andy Graffin (Belgrave H) 14:03; 2 G.Blackman (Harrow) 14:31; 3 C.Davies (Telford) 14:34.

Stage 5. Mark Miles received final instructions from coach Baldaro then tore off down the slope, eager to extend our 500 metre advantage. Fastest long stage man in 2001, Mark had runners to chase that day but now he was the quarry. Morpeth were taken into the frame by Ian Hudspith who closed up a few seconds and Birchfield also improved but the lads from Essex fell away and the Belgrave advantage climbed to 2 minutes 21 seconds as Mark pipped Spencer's time by one second.

1 Belgrave H 1:47:42; 2 Morpeth H 1:50:03; 3 Birchfield H 1:50:20. Fastest: 1 I.Hudspith (Morpeth H) 25:46; 2 M.Miles (Belgrave H) 25:51; 3 D.Burrows (Birchfield) 26:05.

Stage 6. Knowing that he has to be prepared to do battle with the very best to make his mark in the sport, Stephen Sharp was not at all overawed by Andy's earlier 14:03. Birchfield nipped past Morpeth while Tipton were 4th but Steve was on a stormer, his 14:08 being the second fastest up to that point as he opened the gap to nearly three and a half minutes at the halfway point.

1 Belgrave H 2:01:50; 2 Birchfield 2:05:13; 3 Morpeth H 2:05:31. Fastest: 1 S.Sharp (Belgrave H) 14:08; 2 G.Taylor (Trafford AC) 14:24; 3 K.Shepherd (Tipton H) 14:35.

Stage 7. Looking more in command with every run, our National Cross Country silver medallist faced tough opposition on this leg. The Kenyan Kirui had a terrific run for Birchfield but way up the road Allen Graffin was going two seconds faster and another challenge from the Midlanders was turned back.

1 Belgrave H 2:27:35; 2 Birchfield H 2:31:00; 3 Tipton H 2:32:26. Fastest: 1 Allen Graffin (Belgrave H) 25:45; 2 E.Kirui (Birchfield) 25:47; 3 M.Steinle (Blackheath) 25:48.

Stage 8. Determined to make a good showing Charles Herrington probably tackled the initial hill too fast. By the Jamboree stone at the turn for home he had extended our advantage by a further 15 seconds but by the time the climb to the finish was reached he was having to dig very deep and a sub-15 time slipped away although he had dragged the lead out to just under 4 minutes.

1 Belgrave H 2:42:44 ; 2 Birchfield H 2:46:42; 3 Tipton H 2:47:37. Fastest: 1 A.Toward (Morpeth H) 14:47; 2 D.Roache (Trafford AC) 14:51; 3 J.Hayden (Notts AC) 15:01; 5 C.Herrington 15:09.

Stage 9. Veteran of more battles on the road than he'd care to remember, Paul Evans received an ovation before he'd even run a step. "The Belgrave team are so weak," it was announced, "that they are reduced to turning out Paul Evans for stage nine." Inspirational words had come from Paul at the pre-race team talk and now he put his body on the line as only he knows how. On this occasion he was "only" our fourth fastest over the long circuit but he was so intent on giving everything to try to keep the team on course for a fast time that he ran straight through the finish and caught himself up on the ropes separating the incoming and outgoing runners. They don't come any tougher than Paul.

1 Belgrave H 3:08:57; 2 Birchfield H 3:12:41; 3 Morpeth H 3:14:00. Fastest: R.Birchall (Birchfield H) 25:59; 2 M.Hudspith (Morpeth H) 26:01; 3 P.Evans (Belgrave H) 26:13.

Stage 10. Three stages to go. The Belgrave boot had been jammed on the accelerator for over three hours but there was no way it was going to be lifted until the race was complete. Middle-distance man David Anderson fears no-one and had every intention of making that fastest short stage of the day his very own. Not only was it windy, it was getting colder, and yet that 14 minute "barrier" was under threat again. His 14:06 took him to number two and as Birchfield and Morpeth ran men in the high 15s, the Belgrave lead shot up to five and a half minutes. The six minute margin that we had looked for became a real possibility. Tipton came back into the fight for medals, squeezing six seconds ahead of Morpeth.

1 Belgrave H 3:23:03; 2 Birchfield H 3:28:38; 3 Tipton H 3:29:28. Fastest: 1 David Anderson (Belgrave H) 14:06; 2 J.Rogers (Sale H Manchester) 14:31; 3 N.Wilkinson (Salford H) 14:39.

Stage 11. Now Morpeth inched past Tipton again. Down the field Shaftesbury Barnet turned out the first of a powerful finishing duo but it was too late in the day to be of much use. For the Bels Al Stewart was surely on for a 26 minutes "and bits" run but had to be satisfied with grinding out a personal best of just over 27. Birchfield were 8 seconds slower and the gap was now 5 minutes 43 seconds.

1 Belgrave H 3:50:07; 2 Birchfield H 3:55:50; 3 Morpeth H 3:57:03. Fastest: 1 D.Bannister (Shaftesbury Barnet H) 26:28; 2 R.Kay (Notts AC) 26:29; M.Warmby (Newham & Essex B) 26:29; 4 A.Stewart 27:04.

Stage 12. Paul Freary had been strangely quiet ever since he got wind of the fact that he was going to be last stager for us in 2002. The lead was huge but the load was just as heavy. Suppose a kid on a bike sent him sprawling. Suppose Tipton had turned the cattle loose on the far side of the park. No such troubles materialised. Paul took another half minute out of the chasers and he threw his arms aloft in joy as the finish tape was broken.

But it wasn't over yet. Team Captain Roger Alsop had undertaken the awful task of being reserve all day should someone pull a muscle in warm-up. And having done his duty he turned out for Redhill Road Runners who were a man short and languishing somewhere near the tail-end of the field. Just as the medals were being presented, there was Roger, running in an unfamiliar vest and flying up the hill to head all the runners who had taken part in the mass start of tail-enders. A terrific cheer went up from the Belgrave crowd clutching medals and cup and there must have been many a bystander who wondered why on earth Belgrave should be so vociferous and show so much support for Redhill's last man.

1 Belgrave H 4:04:51; 2 Birchfield H 4:11:17; 3 Morpeth H 4:12:12; 4 Tipton H 4:12:45; 5 Cardiff AAC 4:16:50; 6 Bingley H&AC 4:17:45. Fastest: 1 A.Renfree (Shaftesbury Barnet H) 14:39; 2 P.Freary (Belgrave H) 14:44; 3 T.Wall (Morpeth H) 15:09; 5 R.Alsop (Redhill R) 15:18.

Fastest of the day. Long stage: 1 Allen Graffin (Belgrave H) 24:45; 2 I Hudspith (Morpeth H) 25:46; 3 E.Kirui (Birchfield) 25:47; 5 M.Miles 25:51; 6 S.Barden 25:52; 13 P.Evans 26:13; 47 A.Stewart 27:04; =52 L.Hurst 27:13.

Report · page 14

Last gasp victory in opening match

Bill Laws

British Athletics League Division One

Match 1, Watford Stadium, 19 May 2002.

Teams: 1 Belgrave H 355; 2 Newham and Essex Beagles 350; 3 Woodford Green with EL 299; 4 Birchfield H 259; 5 Trafford AC 253; 6 Shaftesbury Barnet H 235.5; 7 Border H 232.5; 8 Harrow AC 211.

Team managers John Jeffery and Bill Laws had predicted that this venue and date would always present a severe test of our strength in depth due to the previous day's televised meet at Loughborough which attracted 20 male and 12 female Belgravians. Out of the 20 men only 5 were able to double up for this meet and the southern team was raided to fill in the gaps.

The Newham team had put out a five line whip and 11 of their Loughborough stars turned up for duty to ensure a hot competition which we seemed destined to lose. But that was not to take account of our young members who competed for every point to ensure a last gasp victory.

The sprints were outstanding with a double win in the 200s for Graham Beasley and Tim Abeyie. That was after an excellent opening 100m from Marlon Dickson who gained a close third in the A event with 10.55 into a 1.8m/s headwind. In the 1500 Andy Graffin followed up his previous day's 800 with a debut in the 1500 which turned out to be a high class and fast tactical race. Andy was surprised at the quality losing out to a fast finishing Matt Shone but was rewarded with a 3.42.8. Steve Sharp was in contention all the way but at 300 to go fell off the pace and jogged the straight. If this hadn't been his first track race he would have held on and looks worth about 3.44 at present. His B string win brought welcome points. Surprisingly our middle distance, which was so strong on the road relays, was weakened by illness, exams and Loughborough to the extent that only Jason Lobo toed the line in the 800. Ian Wells was nominated for the B race but pulled a hammy on the physio's couch. (Poor old felia).

But back to the start where we got maximum points in the 400 hurdles. In the A race Matt Douglas looked strong after recent illness while in the B event 20 year old Andrew Bennett took 1.2 secs off his pb to get the experts thumbing through their books to see who this new talent was. In the high hurdles Neil Owen showed impressive form in the A race to finish second while new member Dominic Girdler got his Belgrave career off to an exciting start with a fine win in the B event.

The throws and jumps which normally give us near max points were good in parts and contrived to ensure that our lead was never more than a few points throughout the afternoon until event 16 when we seemed to be easing towards a clear win. At this point several of the tired Newham relay runners who had performed valiantly over two days called a halt little realising that the triple jump and steeplechase results would bring us almost level as we approached the final sprint and long relays. And it was our young up and coming youngsters who once more performed above expectations to secure a close victory with two first rate performances.

Report · page 15

Track boys swing it again

Bill Laws

British Athletics League Division One

Match 2, Birmingham, 6 July 2002.

1 Belgrave H 382; 2 Newham and Essex Beagles 345; 3 Birchfield H 340; 4 Woodford Green with EL 305; 5 Trafford AC 242; 6 Shaftesbury Barnet H 210; 7 Border H & AC 187; 8 Harrow AC 161.

Another match, another format. Billed as a domestic Grand Prix and supported by UK Athletics with prize money and guests in certain events we waited to see how the fixture and our fortunes would pan out. After match 1, where we sneaked a last gasp win, we feared Newham, who have the desire and ability to beat all but our very best team. Missing for the first time in 4 years as he opened his son's school fete was Mick Jones so usual key points would be lost in the hammer. Added to this an injury to pole vault supremo Paul Williamson plus a total loss of our top steeplechasers meant that we would have at least three duff events. Would our increasing strength on the track and our remaining throwers be enough to defeat the opposition?

Well yes. A series of truly dominating performances from 100m to 5000m contributed to a clear win which put us in a slightly more comfortable lead as we chase a sixth consecutive and record breaking League victory.

Highlights of a series of top rate performances were Andy Graffin's gun to tape charge in the A 800. Adam Zawadski got caught up in traffic jams on the M6 and being a fairly local lad arrived only 15 minutes before the A 800 and ran a pb in his first 800 of the season. An A and B max points win echoed by Steve Sharp and Spencer Barden in the 1500. Plus near max points in the 5000 to follow. Paul Evans took the race out to be caught by Kirui but his A silver was followed by a B gold and a big pb by Alaster Stewart. Brilliant stuff.

In the short hurdles Neil Owen was close to beating Commonwealth bound Damian Greaves while Dominic Girdler ensured more good points with a B win. In the long hurdles Matt Douglas experimented with a new stride pattern from 7 and tied up badly to succumb to a 49.4 win by WG's Kemel Thompson (Jamaican) who finished 6th in the last Commonwealth Games. In the 200 Graham Beasley again won the A and Tim Abeyie claimed the famous scalp of former Belgravian Chris Rawlinson who now competes for Trafford.

But it was the 400 that set the pulses racing. On the start line Sean Baldock faced no less an adversary than Iwan Thomas seeking proof of a return to fitness prior to next week's AAA's. And it was Sean hitting the lead at 200 who held on well for a stunning victory in a sub 46 timing which was his fastest since the Sydney Olympics. He followed this with a 45.5 split in the long relay. A great afternoon's work!

In general the jumps were below par but Ben Challenger cleared 2.20 to win the A high jump and prove once again that when he puts on a Belgrave vest you can expect something special. One of his 2.26 attempts was pretty close. But it was the triple jump that yielded the biggest surprise. Last year junior David Wellstead won the B and was headed by promising junior Seni Mayekodunmi who contributed to a 20 points total out of a max 28 for this event.

So a clear win. The debate on the new format started as soon as the last event finished. With the inclusion of guests and prize money down to third place in most events this could be the league format of the future. Or is this another false dawn?

For Grand Prix events actual positions shown in brackets.

Report · page 15

A knockout performance !

Bill Laws

Match 3, Copthall, 3 August 2002.

1 Belgrave H 386; 2 Woodford Green with EL 317; 3 Birchfield H 301; 4 Trafford AC 289; 5 Shaftesbury Barnet H 272; 6 Newham & Essex Beagles 266; 7 Border H & AC 173; 8 Harrow AC 162.

Match 3 was squeezed in between the Commonwealth Games and European Championships and we were fortunate that Richard Aspden, Matt Douglas and Paul Williamson returned from Manchester and made the effort to support this key fixture. Also adding to our potential problems was an under 23 match in France which took away a further five of our regular team. Plus the absence of our top hammer men which meant we would be off to a rocky start. But it became even rockier when with 20 minutes to go we had no 400 hurdlers due to traffic hold ups. Fortunately Noel Levy just made it but Andrew Bennett didn't. Andrew made amends by running a PB in the B flat 400.

The unique sight of Kevin Brown and Lee Newman in the hammer was of interest to the opposition but resulted in few points with the result that after five events we were last and Trafford were leading. But then the track men got going with near max points from the 200 through to the 5000. Highlights were: season's debuts from Chris Moss to win the A 800 and Richard Ward to win the B 1500 behind Spencer Barden who ran a season's best. Steve Sharp ran a big PB in the A 5000 while Paul Freary won the B. It was good to see Kevin Nash return from injury in the 3000 steeplechase while Will Cockerell ran a big PB in the B 'chase squeezing under 10 minutes for the first time.

Maximum points from Neil Owen and Richard Sear in the sprint hurdles plus a second place in the sprint relay and a win in the long relay moved us into an unassailable lead. Ben Flint gained a win over Paul Williamson in the pole vault to ensure near maximum points and further light between us and second placed Woodford Green.

Looking at the final scores it may be difficult to believe that the team mangers were stressed throughout the afternoon as first Stuart Faben was delayed too by traffic and only arrived as the third round of the javelin was under way and Neil Owen didn't help by getting the start time for the sprint relay wrong. Geoff Dearman who had warmed up for the long relay virtually leaped into the lead slot to ensure a good second place. Matt Douglas fittingly concluded the day with a fine 46.9 anchor leg in the long relay to follow a good afternoon's work including a winning 21.80 B 200.
